Your primary role is to assist users by executing commands, modifying code, and solving technical problems effectively. You should be thorough, methodical, and prioritize quality over speed.

</EFFICIENCY>

<FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>
* When a user provides a file path, do NOT assume it's relative to the current working directory. First explore the file system to locate the file before working on it.
* If asked to edit a file, edit the file directly, rather than creating a new file with a different filename.
* For global search-and-replace operations, consider using `sed` instead of opening file editors multiple times.
* NEVER create multiple versions of the same file with different suffixes (e.g., file_test.py, file_fix.py, file_simple.py). Instead:

  - If you need to create a temporary file for testing, delete it once you've confirmed your solution works

* Do NOT include documentation files explaining your changes in version control unless the user explicitly requests it
* When reproducing bugs or implementing fixes, use a single file rather than creating multiple files with different versions
</FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>

<CODE_QUALITY>
* Write clean, efficient code with minimal comments. Avoid redundancy in comments: Do not repeat information that can be easily inferred from the code itself.
* When implementing solutions, focus on making the minimal changes needed to solve the problem.
* Before implementing any changes, first thoroughly understand the codebase through exploration.

The issue describes a problem with **extra whitespace** in the second line of **maxed nested lists** in markdown processing. 

**Technical Details:**
- **Method names:** `test_nested_three_block_max_block_max_ordered_max_no_bq1`, `test_nested_three_block_max_block_max_unordered_max_no_bq1`
- **Expected output:** `>       item` (8 spaces before the >)
- **Actual output:** `>       item` (16 spaces before the >)
- **Context:** Blockquote (`>`) nested within maximum-depth list structures

### 1.4 Steps to Reproduce
1. Create a deeply nested markdown structure involving blockquotes and maximum-depth lists
2. Process this structure through the pymarkdown parser
3. Examine the indentation of the second line in the output
4. Observe that there are twice as many leading spaces as expected

### 1.5 Best Practices
- Make minimal changes to indentation calculation logic
- Preserve existing functionality for other nesting scenarios
- Test both ordered and unordered list variants
- Follow existing code patterns and conventions

    23	
    24	PyMarkdown is primarily a Markdown Linter.  To ensure that the Markdown
    25	[linting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lint_%28software%29)
    26	is accomplished successfully, the rules engine that powers the linter
    27	uses a Markdown parser that is both
    28	[GitHub Flavored Markdown](https://github.github.com/gfm/)
    29	compliant and
    30	[CommonMark](https://spec.commonmark.org/)
    31	compliant.  The rules provided in the base application can be easily extended
    32	by writing new plugins and importing them into the rules engine through simple
    33	configuration options.
    34	
    35	The PyMarkdown project has the following advantages:
    36	
    37	- Consistency
    38	  - This project can examine multiple files and directories with one invocation,
    39	    ensuring that all detected Markdown files adhere to the same guidelines.
    40	- Portable
    41	  - The linter runs on any system running Python 3.8 or later, with no modifications.
    42	- Standardized
    43	  - The parser that powers the linter is GitHub Flavored Markdown (GFM) compliant.
    44	    Due to that foundation, the parser does not guess how some parsers may handle
    45	    a given situation, as it has a clear set of rules to follow.
    46	- Accurate
    47	  - The parser passes all GFM conformance tests and CommonMark conformance tests.  In
    48	    test scenarios that were not present in either set of tests, the CommonMark
    49	    0.29.2 release was used to determine the correct parsing.
    50	- Flexible
    51	  - Each Markdown document is parsed into an internal token format.  Most of the rules
    52	    and made more efficient by leveraging this token format. Where that is not possible,
    53	    simple regular expressions and simple algorithms are used on a line-by-line basis.
    54	- Thoroughly tested
    55	  - The project currently has over 3200 scenario tests and coverage percentages
    56	    over 99%.
    57	- Extensible
    58	  - The parser for the project adheres to the GFM specification and most of
    59	    the rules for the parser leverage the tokens produced by that parser. The
    60	    [rules](/docs/rules.md) themselves are plugins, so they are extensible by default.
    61	    The parser itself will be extended as needed to provide for other Markdown
    62	    [extensions](/docs/extensions.md) as needed.
    63	- Versatile
    64	  - The PyMarkdown linter can be executed from a script on the command line,
    65	    from within another Python program, or using the popular Git Pre-Commit hooks.
    66	
    67	## Note
    68	
    69	This project is currently in beta, and some of these documented things
    70	may not work 100% as advertised until after the final release.  However,
    71	everything should be close enough to done that if you find something missing,
    72	please let us know.
    73	
    74	## GitHub Pre Commit Hooks
    75	
    76	If you intend to use the PyMarkdown project as a linter for your
    77	GitHub Pre-Commit hooks, then refer to [this document](/docs/pre-commit.md)
    78	on how to set that up.  Once setup, you can continue reading at the
    79	[How To Use section](#how-to-use) for more information on the options
    80	available for use in your Pre-Commit Hooks.
    81	
    82	## Requirements
    83	
    84	This project required Python 3.8 or later to function.
    85	
    86	## Installation
    87	
    88	```text
    89	pip install pymarkdownlnt
    90	```
    91	
    92	## How To Use
    93	
    94	### If You Get Stuck
    95	
    96	Full help support is available by entering
    97	
    98	```shell
    99	pymarkdown --help
   100	```
   101	
   102	on the command line and pressing enter.  For an individual command,
   103	help is available by following the command or commands with `--help`
   104	as follows:
   105	
   106	```shell
   107	pymarkdown scan --help
   108	```
   109	
   110	### Prerequisites
   111	
   112	Various sections of this document benefit from having concrete examples
   113	to illustrate how things work. For the following sections,
   114	this documentation will assume that there is a file called `example-1.md`
   115	in a directory called `examples` that has the following content:
   116	
   117	```Markdown
   118	## This is an example
   119	
   120	Just an example.
   121	```
   122	
   123	and a file called `example-2.md` in that same directory that has the
   124	following content:
   125	
   126	```Markdown
   127	# This is an example
   128	
   129	Just an example.
   130	
   131	```
   132	
   133	If you prefer concrete files, these files are checked into the
   134	[examples directory](https://github.com/jackdewinter/pymarkdown/tree/main/examples) of the GitHub project.
   135	
   136	### Basic Scanning
   137	
   138	The PyMarkdown linter is executed by calling the project from the
   139	command line and
   140	specifying one or more files and directories to scan for Markdown `.md`
   141	files.  The set of files and/or directories must be prefaced with the
   142	`scan` keyword to denote that scanning is required. For the examples
   143	directory, both this form:
   144	
   145	```shell
   146	pymarkdown scan examples
   147	```
   148	
   149	and this form:
   150	
   151	```shell
   152	pymarkdown scan examples/example-1.md examples/example-2.md
   153	```
   154	
   155	can be used to scan both files in the directory.  The only difference
   156	between the two invocations is that the first example will scan every
   157	Markdown `.md` file in the `examples` directory, while the second
   158	invocation will only scan the two specified files.  For clarity purposes,
   159	if the command line specifies the same file multiple times, that file
   160	name will only be added to the list of files to scan once.
   161	
   162	If everything is working properly, both of the above scans will
   163	produce the following output:
   164	
   165	```text
   166	examples/example-1.md:3:16: MD047: Each file should end with a single newline character. (single-trailing-newline)
   167	```
   168	
   169	### Rules
   170	
   171	The PyMarkdown project includes 13 out-of-the-box [rules](/docs/rules.md),
   172	with another 29 rules to be added before the
   173	1.0.0 release.  These rules are implemented using a simple plugin
   174	system that is documented in the [developer documentation](/docs/developer.md).
   175	It is these rules that allow the PyMarkdown project to scan
   176	the various Markdown files, looking for bad patterns over that set of
   177	Markdown documents.
   178	
   179	Because of the way that the rules are provided, sometimes we
   180	refer to the rules as `rules` and sometimes as `rule plugins`.  A `rule`
   181	is a specific set of conditions that trigger the reporting of a violation
   182	when those conditions occur.  A `rule plugin` is the Python class
   183	and Python file in which the `rule` is supplied to the PyMarkdown application.
   184	Our goal is to try to not use these phrases interchangeably, but we are only
   185	human.  If we do mess up and use the wrong phase, we do apologize.
   186	
   187	Note that the initial set of rules are modelled after the 42 rules provided by
   188	David Anson's [Markdown Lint](https://github.com/markdownlint/markdownlint)
   189	project.  This decision was made
   190	to give Markdown authors that use his project in their IDEs (such as
   191	the MarkdownLint plugin for VSCode that I use), a good grounding
   192	in what they can consistently check for.
   193	
   194	### Rule Violation Format
   195	
   196	Executing either of the above example command lines will produce the following output:
   197	
   198	```text
   199	/examples/example-1.md:3:16: MD047: Each file should end with a single newline character. (single-trailing-newline)
   200	```
   201	
   202	The format of the output for any rules that are triggered is as follows:
   203	
   204	`file-name:line:column: rule-id: description (aliases)`
   205	
   206	- `file-name` - Path to the file that triggered the rule.
   207	- `line`/`column` - Position in the file where the rule was triggered.
   208	- `rule-id` - Unique identifier assigned to the rule.
   209	- `description` - Human readable description of the rule.
   210	- `aliases` - One or more aliases used to reference the rule.
   211	
   212	For the rule violation that was reported at the start of this section,
   213	the first step in diagnosing
   214	that violation is to look at the file `/examples/example-1.md` at the end of
   215	line 3, which is column 16.  Rule [md047](/docs/rules/rule_md047.md) specifies
   216	that every file should end with a single newline character, which is
   217	what is reported in the violation's description.  Additionally, it reports that this
   218	rule can also be identified by the more human readable alias of
   219	`single-trailing-newline`.
   220	
   221	### Basic Configuration
   222	
   223	The most frequently used part of the configuration system is the
   224	part that enables and disables specific rules while scanning the
   225	Markdown files.  For example, if you do not like rule md047 which
   226	states that each file must end with a single newline, you can
   227	disable that rule by specifying:
   228	
   229	```shell
   230	pymarkdown -d md047 scan /examples
   231	```
   232	
   233	or:
   234	
   235	```shell
   236	pymarkdown --disable-rules md047 scan /examples
   237	```
   238	
   239	The effect of disabling the rule should be evidenced by
   240	the scan no longer reporting any violations of rule md047
   241	against the Markdown file `example-1.md`.
   242	
   243	Alternatively, rules can also be enabled.  As the modelled
   244	base rules for this project are based off those rules for David
   245	Anson's project, rule md002 is disable by default in both
   246	projects.  Specifically, rule md002 is disabled by default
   247	as rule md041 provides a better implementation of that rule
   248	that takes front-matter into account.  Until that rule is
   249	implemented, you can enable rule md002 by specifying either:
   250	
   251	```shell
   252	pymarkdown -e md002 scan /examples
   253	```
   254	
   255	or
   256	
   257	```shell
   258	pymarkdown --enable-rules md002 scan /examples
   259	```
   260	
   261	The effect of enabling the rule is evidenced by
   262	the scan reporting a violation of Rule md002 against
   263	Markdown file `example-1.md`:
   264	
   265	```text
   266	examples/example-1.md:1:1: MD002: First heading of the document should be a top level heading.
   267	 [Expected: h1; Actual: h2] (first-heading-h1, first-header-h1)
   268	examples/example-1.md:3:16: MD047: Each file should end with a single newline character.
   269	(single-trailing-newline)
   270	```
   271	
   272	### Extensions
   273	
   274	Extensions are any features that are implemented in addition to the base
   275	[GitHub Flavored Markdown](https://github.github.com/gfm/) specification.
   276	These extensions are documented in the [extensions](/docs/extensions.md)
   277	document, including information about how they perform, details on the
   278	extension, and configuration information.
   279

   301	## Open Issues and Future Plans
   302	
   303	During the development phase of this project, it was more useful to have
   304	an actual list of issues to track and prioritize, rather than relying on
   305	GitHub to do all the work. This is the location of the prioritized
   306	[Issues List](/issues.md).
   307	
   308	If you find any issues, please report them using the standard GitHub
   309	issues process.  When our team looks at your issue and triages
   310	it, it will be added to our Issues List with the triaged priority.
   311	For us, this provides transparency as to what we are currently working
   312	on, what is up next, and what our plans are for further development.

   324	
   325	If you would like to report an issue with the linter, a rule, or
   326	the documentation, please file an issue [using GitHub](https://github.com/jackdewinter/pymarkdown/issues).
   327	
   328	If you would like to us to implement a feature that you believe is
   329	important, please file an issue
   330	[using GitHub](https://github.com/jackdewinter/pymarkdown/issues)
   331	that includes what you want to add, why you want to add it,
   332	and why it is important.  Please note that the issue will usually be
   333	the start of a conversation, and be ready for more questions.
   334	
   335	If you would like to contribute to the project in a more
   336	substantial manner, please contact me at `jack.de.winter` at `outlook.com`.

    12	def test_nested_three_block_block_unordered():

    14	    Verify that a nesting of block quote, block quote, unordered list works

    @pytest.mark.gfm
    def test_nested_three_block_max_block_max_ordered_max_no_bq1():
        """
        Verify that a nesting of block quote, block quote, ordered list, with
        the maximum number of spaces allowed works  properly,
        with no block quote characters on the second line.
        """

        # Arrange
        source_markdown = """   >    >    1. list
            >       item"""
        expected_tokens = [
            "[block-quote(1,4):   :   > ]",
            "[block-quote(1,9)::   >    > \n]",
            "[olist(1,14):.:1:16:   :        ]",

    def verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
        """
        Verify that we can use the information in the tokens to do a round trip back
        to the original Markdown that created the token.
        """

        if "\t" in source_markdown:
            new_source = []
            split_source = source_markdown.split(ParserHelper.newline_character)
            for next_line in split_source:
                if "\t" in next_line:
                    next_line = ParserHelper.detabify_string(next_line)
                new_source.append(next_line)
            source_markdown = ParserHelper.newline_character.join(new_source)

        transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
        original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)

        print(
            "".join(
                [
                    "\n-=-=-\nExpected\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\nActual\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\n",
                ]
            )
        )
        diff = difflib.ndiff(source_markdown, original_markdown)
        diff_values = "".join(
            [
                "\n-=-=-n",
                ParserHelper.newline_character.join(list(diff)),
                "\n-=-=-expected\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-actual\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-\n",
            ]
        )

>       assert (
            source_markdown == original_markdown
        ), f"Markdown strings are not equal.{diff_values}"

import sys
import os
sys.path.insert(0, os.path.join(os.path.dirname(__file__), '.'))

from pymarkdown.tokenized_markdown import TokenizedMarkdown
from test.transform_to_markdown import TransformToMarkdown

def test_issue_reproduction():
    """Reproduce the issue with extra whitespace in nested block quotes."""
    
    # Test case from the issue
    source_markdown = """   >    >    1. list
        >       item"""
    
    print("Source markdown:")
    print(repr(source_markdown))
    print()
    
    # Parse the markdown
    tokenizer = TokenizedMarkdown()
    actual_tokens = tokenizer.transform(source_markdown)
    
    print("Parsed tokens:")
    for i, token in enumerate(actual_tokens):
        print(f"{i:2}: {token}")
    print()
    
    # Transform back to markdown
    transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
    original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)
    
    print("Original markdown:")
    print(repr(original_markdown))
    print()
    
    print("Comparison:")
    print(f"Expected: {repr(source_markdown)}")
    print(f"Actual:   {repr(original_markdown)}")
    print(f"Equal:    {source_markdown == original_markdown}")
    
    if source_markdown != original_markdown:
        print("\nDifference analysis:")
        expected_lines = source_markdown.split('\n')
        actual_lines = original_markdown.split('\n')
        
        for i, (exp, act) in enumerate(zip(expected_lines, actual_lines)):
            if exp != act:
                print(f"Line {i+1}:")
                print(f"  Expected: {repr(exp)}")
                print(f"  Actual:   {repr(act)}")
                
                # Show character by character
                print("  Char diff:")
                for j, (e_char, a_char) in enumerate(zip(exp, act)):
                    if e_char != a_char:
                        print(f"    Pos {j}: expected '{e_char}' ({ord(e_char)}) got '{a_char}' ({ord(a_char)})")
                if len(exp) != len(act):
                    print(f"    Length difference: expected {len(exp)}, got {len(act)}")

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS: Understanding the Problem<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>Now I can clearly see what's happening:

1. The source markdown has:
   - Line 1: `   >    >    1. list` (3 spaces + ">    >    1. list")
   - Line 2: `        >       item` (8 spaces + ">       item")

2. During parsing, the tokens are correctly generated with the right indentation levels.

3. During transformation back to markdown, the issue occurs in the `__adjust_for_list` method.

Looking at the debug trace:
- In line 2 processing, it goes through `__adjust_for_list` 
- It finds a nested block quote at index 1 (`nested_block_start_index->1`)
- It applies the leading spaces from that block quote token
- But it's applying them twice - once in `__adjust_for_list` and once elsewhere

The key insight is in this part of the debug:
```
looking for nested block start
nested_block_start_index->1:previous_token=[block-quote(1,9)::   >    > \n]
token_stack[-1]=[olist(1,14):.:1:16:   :        ]
applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line->True
-->container_line>        >       item
-->container_line>                >       item
```

The condition `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line->True` means that leading spaces were already applied, but then it applies them again.

Looking at the logic in `__adjust_for_list`:
```python
if (
    applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
    or token_stack[-1].line_number != previous_token.line_number
):
```

The issue is that even when `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is True, it's still applying the leading spaces again. This condition should probably be:
```python
if (
    not applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
    and token_stack[-1].line_number != previous_token.line_number
):
```

Or the logic needs to be adjusted so that when `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is True, it doesn't re-apply the same spaces.</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think>### 5.1 Problem Statement
The issue is that in the transformation from tokens back to markdown, when processing continuation lines in deeply nested block quotes with lists, the indentation is being applied twice, resulting in double the expected whitespace.

### 5.2 Problem Location  
The problem is in the `__adjust_for_list` method in `/workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9/test/transform_to_markdown.py` around lines 509-512.

### 5.3 How the Test Reproduces the Issue
The test creates a markdown structure with maximum nesting:
- First line: `   >    >    1. list` (properly indented)
- Second line: `        >       item` (should remain with 8 spaces)

During transformation, the second line gets processed and the block quote leading spaces get applied twice, turning 8 spaces into 16 spaces.

### 5.4 Best Practices for the Fix
- Make minimal changes to fix the double-application of indentation
- Ensure the fix doesn't break other nested structures
- Preserve existing functionality for correctly working cases
- Test both ordered and unordered list variants

### 5.5 How to Fix the Problem
The issue is in the condition logic in `__adjust_for_list`. When `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is `True`, it means the leading spaces have already been applied, so we shouldn't apply them again. The current logic applies them regardless.

The fix is to modify the condition to only apply leading spaces when they haven't already been applied.

This is happening in `__apply_primary_transformation`. The line comes in with 8 spaces (`        >       item`) and comes out with 16 spaces (`                >       item`).

This means that in `__apply_primary_transformation`, the leading spaces are being applied even when they should not be. Let me check the logic there.

Looking at the code in `__apply_primary_transformation`:

```python
applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line = not (
    did_move_ahead and current_changed_record[0]
)
```

And then if `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is True, it applies the leading spaces.

In our case:
- `did_move_ahead` is False (we can see that in the debug)
- `current_changed_record` is None (we can see that in the debug: `-->current_changed_record>None`)

So `not (False and None[0])` evaluates to `not (False)` which is `True`.

Therefore `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` becomes `True`, and the leading spaces get applied.

But wait, that's not right. If `current_changed_record` is None, we can't access `current_changed_record[0]`. Let me check what's happening.

[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Perfect! Now I can see the exact issue:

For the second line:
```
DEBUG __apply_primary_transformation: did_move_ahead=False, current_changed_record=None
DEBUG: applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line=True
```

So `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` becomes `True` because:
- `did_move_ahead` is `False`
- `current_changed_record` is `None` (which is falsy)
- So `did_move_ahead and current_changed_record and current_changed_record[0]` becomes `False and None and None[0]` which is `False` (short-circuit evaluation prevents `None[0]`)
- Therefore `not (False)` becomes `True`

And then the leading spaces get applied, causing the double indentation.

The fix is that we should NOT apply leading spaces when `current_changed_record` is `None`.     def verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
        """
        Verify that we can use the information in the tokens to do a round trip back
        to the original Markdown that created the token.
        """

        if "\t" in source_markdown:
            new_source = []
            split_source = source_markdown.split(ParserHelper.newline_character)
            for next_line in split_source:
                if "\t" in next_line:
                    next_line = ParserHelper.detabify_string(next_line)
                new_source.append(next_line)
            source_markdown = ParserHelper.newline_character.join(new_source)

        transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
        original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)

        print(
            "".join(
                [
                    "\n-=-=-\nExpected\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\nActual\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\n",
                ]
            )
        )
        diff = difflib.ndiff(source_markdown, original_markdown)
        diff_values = "".join(
            [
                "\n-=-=-n",
                ParserHelper.newline_character.join(list(diff)),
                "\n-=-=-expected\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-actual\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-\n",
            ]
        )

The issue is not that we should never apply leading spaces when `current_changed_record` is `None`, but rather that we're applying them in a specific scenario where they've already been applied.

Let me think about this more carefully. Looking at the debug from the original failing test:

In the working case (before my fix):
1. Line 1 gets processed normally
2. Line 2: `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` becomes `True` 
3. Then in `__adjust_for_list`, the condition was `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line or line_number_check` which meant it applied leading spaces even though they were already applied

In the broken case (the test I just broke):
1. Line 1 gets processed normally  
2. Line 2: `current_changed_record` is `None`, so we need to apply leading spaces to get the proper prefix

The key insight is that I need to distinguish between:
1. Cases where leading spaces were already applied by `__apply_primary_transformation` and shouldn't be applied again in `__adjust_for_list`
2. Cases where leading spaces need to be applied in `__adjust_for_list` because they weren't applied in `__apply_primary_transformation`

Let me look at the logic more carefully. The real issue is in `__adjust_for_list`. The condition there was:
```python
if (
    applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
    or token_stack[-1].line_number != previous_token.line_number
):
```

This meant: "Apply leading spaces if they were already applied OR if line numbers don't match"

That's clearly wrong. It should be: "Apply leading spaces if they WEREN'T already applied AND line numbers don't match".

But wait, let me double-check this understanding by looking at a working case. The problem is that my condition is too restrictive. In Case 2, we DO need to apply leading spaces in `__adjust_for_list` even when they weren't applied in `__apply_primary_transformation`.

[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>I can see:
1. **Line 1**: `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line->False`
2. **Line 2**: `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line->True`  

But then it shows:
```
-->container_line>item
-->container_line>  item
```

So it's applying SOME leading spaces (just 2 spaces instead of `> >   `). This suggests that the issue is not that we're preventing ALL leading space application, but that we're applying the wrong leading spaces.

Let me think about this differently. The core issue is that we have TWO places where leading spaces can be applied:
1. `__apply_primary_transformation` 
2. `__adjust_for_list`

The problem is that sometimes both places apply leading spaces to the same line, causing duplication.

But in the working case, we NEED one of them to apply the spaces. The key is figuring out WHEN each should apply.

Looking at the logic:
- `__apply_primary_transformation` applies leading spaces when `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is True
- `__adjust_for_list` should apply leading spaces when `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is False AND line numbers don't match

But there's a third consideration: what about the case where neither applies but we still need the leading spaces?

Let me look at this from a different angle. Maybe the issue is that the condition in `__apply_primary_transformation` is wrong, not the condition in `__adjust_for_list`.

Let me check what the original condition was supposed to mean. Looking at the logic more carefully:

In `__apply_primary_transformation`:
```python
applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line = not (
    did_move_ahead and current_changed_record[0]
)
```

This means: Apply leading spaces if we did NOT move ahead AND the current record is not a start record.

But in our failing case:
- `did_move_ahead` = False
- `current_changed_record` = None
- So `did_move_ahead and current_changed_record[0]` = `False and None[0]` 

Wait, that would crash! But it's not crashing. Let me check what the actual logic is doing.

Looking at the debug from the broken test:
```
-->did_move_ahead>False
-->current_changed_record>None
```

So `did_move_ahead and current_changed_record[0]` should be `False and None[0]` which should crash when trying to access `None[0]`.

But it's not crashing. This means the condition is being evaluated differently. Let me check if there's short-circuit evaluation protection:

In Python, `False and None[0]` won't evaluate `None[0]` because of short-circuit evaluation. So it becomes `False`, and `not False` becomes `True`.

So `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` becomes `True` even when `current_changed_record` is `None`.

This is the bug! When `current_changed_record` is `None`, we shouldn't be applying leading spaces in `__apply_primary_transformation` at all.

The correct logic should be:
```python
applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line = (
    current_changed_record is not None 
    and not (did_move_ahead and current_changed_record[0])
)
```

This means: Apply leading spaces only when there IS a current changed record, AND we did not move ahead with a start record.

    def verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
        """
        Verify that we can use the information in the tokens to do a round trip back
        to the original Markdown that created the token.
        """

        if "\t" in source_markdown:
            new_source = []
            split_source = source_markdown.split(ParserHelper.newline_character)
            for next_line in split_source:
                if "\t" in next_line:
                    next_line = ParserHelper.detabify_string(next_line)
                new_source.append(next_line)
            source_markdown = ParserHelper.newline_character.join(new_source)

        transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
        original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)

        print(
            "".join(
                [
                    "\n-=-=-\nExpected\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\nActual\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\n",
                ]
            )
        )
        diff = difflib.ndiff(source_markdown, original_markdown)
        diff_values = "".join(
            [
                "\n-=-=-n",
                ParserHelper.newline_character.join(list(diff)),
                "\n-=-=-expected\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-actual\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-\n",
            ]
        )

    def verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
        """
        Verify that we can use the information in the tokens to do a round trip back
        to the original Markdown that created the token.
        """

        if "\t" in source_markdown:
            new_source = []
            split_source = source_markdown.split(ParserHelper.newline_character)
            for next_line in split_source:
                if "\t" in next_line:
                    next_line = ParserHelper.detabify_string(next_line)
                new_source.append(next_line)
            source_markdown = ParserHelper.newline_character.join(new_source)

        transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
        original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)

        print(
            "".join(
                [
                    "\n-=-=-\nExpected\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\nActual\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\n",
                ]
            )
        )
        diff = difflib.ndiff(source_markdown, original_markdown)
        diff_values = "".join(
            [
                "\n-=-=-n",
                ParserHelper.newline_character.join(list(diff)),
                "\n-=-=-expected\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-actual\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-\n",
            ]
        )

            source_markdown == original_markdown

    def verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
        """
        Verify that we can use the information in the tokens to do a round trip back
        to the original Markdown that created the token.
        """

        if "\t" in source_markdown:
            new_source = []
            split_source = source_markdown.split(ParserHelper.newline_character)
            for next_line in split_source:
                if "\t" in next_line:
                    next_line = ParserHelper.detabify_string(next_line)
                new_source.append(next_line)
            source_markdown = ParserHelper.newline_character.join(new_source)

        transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
        original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)

        print(
            "".join(
                [
                    "\n-=-=-\nExpected\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\nActual\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\n",
                ]
            )
        )
        diff = difflib.ndiff(source_markdown, original_markdown)
        diff_values = "".join(
            [
                "\n-=-=-n",
                ParserHelper.newline_character.join(list(diff)),
                "\n-=-=-expected\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-actual\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-\n",
            ]
        )

Let me think about this differently. What makes the two cases different?

**Original issue case**:
- Line 1: `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` = `False` (correct)
- Line 2: `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` = `True` (problematic - leading spaces already applied)

**Broken test case**:  
- Line 1: `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` = `False` (correct)
- Line 2: `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` = `False` (needs leading spaces applied)

So the difference is in line 2:
- Original issue: leading spaces were already applied, so we shouldn't apply them again  
- Broken test: leading spaces were NOT applied, so we SHOULD apply them

The problem is that the line number check prevents applying them in the broken test case.

But the line number check is there for a reason - to prevent applying leading spaces inappropriately.

Maybe the real issue is that I need to understand WHAT constitutes a "continuation line" vs a "new element line".

Looking at the tokens:
**Original issue**:
```
[olist(1,14):.:1:16:   :        ]
[para(1,17):\n        ]
[text(1,17):list\n>       item::\n]
```

**Broken test**:  
```
[ulist(1,5):+::6::  ]  
[para(1,7):\n]
[text(1,7):list\nitem::\n]
```

In the original issue, the text contains `>       item` which already has the block quote marker.
In the broken test, the text contains just `item` without the block quote marker.

This suggests that the real distinction is whether the content already contains the block quote markers or not.

But that's determined during parsing, not during transformation.

Let me approach this from a different angle. Maybe the issue is that I'm being too conservative with the `__apply_primary_transformation` fix.
